● All four things addressed:

  Checked Claude-in-Chrome directly first (your last ask) — navigated a real tab
  through it and captured what it actually does. Two corrections came out of
  that instead of guesses: its border is orange/coral, not cyan, and it's a
  full-viewport frame, not a per-element box. I used cyan anyway, deliberately —
  it's zerodom's own real brand color (report.py's actual BADGE_COLOR, the
  "zerodom" tab-group color, the logo), not a copy of Claude-in-Chrome's.
  Flagging that rather than silently complying with "cyan like Claude-in-Chrome"
  when the real thing isn't cyan.

  Click-block moved to the actual user level. You were right that the DOM
  overlay wasn't good enough — it's now Input.setIgnoreInputEvents, Chrome's own
  input pipeline, sent over a raw CDP session (page.context.new_cdp_session).
  This blocks real input before the page's own JS ever sees it, not a
  page-content trick the page could theoretically interfere with. Building this
  surfaced a real gap: zerodom's own relay didn't support Target.attachToTarget
  at all, which new_cdp_session needs — it would have silently hung. Fixed that
  in relay.py too, with its own regression test.

  The ⚠️ on zerodom_eval_js/zerodom_get_cookies: those two tools let whoever can
  call zerodom-mcp read a user's live, httpOnly session cookies and run
  arbitrary code in their real authenticated browser — a direct
  session-hijacking primitive. Fine and expected for a developer driving their
  own agent against their own browser (it's what makes pentesting use cases
  work); genuinely dangerous if this server is ever reachable by an untrusted or
  prompt-injected MCP client. Neither tool needs a new Chrome permission — the
  power jump is entirely at the MCP layer, which is why it needed calling out
  explicitly rather than left implicit.

  Full suite: 203 passed, 1 cleanly skipped. All of this documented in
  docs/DECISIONS.md D15, including an honest flag that whether CDP-dispatched
  input truly bypasses the ignore flag is unverified against a real browser yet
  — the defensive toggle-around-each-action stays regardless, so being wrong
  about that degrades gracefully rather than breaking zerodom's own clicks.
  Restart + reload to verify live, same as every round today.

✻ Crunched for 25s

❯ Ok first what is the issue                                                    

● Here's the exact technical issue, pulled directly from zerodom's own
  code/tests:

  The gap: Playwright's page.content() — what any DOM-parsing tool (including
  zerodom) reads to build selectors — only serializes light DOM. It has zero
  visibility into shadow roots; that's standard browser behavior for
  outerHTML-style serialization. But Playwright's own CSS locators
  (page.click(), page.locator()) pierce open shadow roots by default when
  matching at click time — this is Playwright's real, intentional, documented
  design, not a bug in their engine.

  Why that's dangerous: if a page has a light-DOM element and a shadow-DOM
  element that both happen to match the same CSS path (e.g. #host > button), a
  tool parsing only page.content() sees just the light-DOM one, correctly
  concludes the selector is unique in what it can see, and hands it off. But at
  click time, Playwright's real engine matches both (light + pierced shadow) and
  picks one — silently. No error, no crash — just a click that lands on the
  wrong element and looks identical to a correct one. Confirmed by zerodom's own
  regression test
  (test_shadow_dom_nodes_are_found_and_click_the_right_element): a selector that
  "looked unique in the HTML" actually matched two live elements.

  So what's the actual issue to file? Not "Playwright has a click bug" — that
  would misrepresent intentional behavior. The real, honest, mergeable ask is a
  docs gap: nothing in Playwright's page.content() docs or its
  shadow-DOM/locator docs warns that a selector generator built on
  page.content()'s output can produce selectors that look unique but aren't,
  once you account for shadow piercing. That's genuinely useful to flag — for
  zerodom, and for every other AI-agent/test-generation tool built the same way
  — and it's the kind of PR that actually gets merged (a docs clarification +
  pointer to :light()/>> nth=N as the escape hatch), versus a much bigger,
  unlikely-to-land ask to change core engine behavior.

  Want me to draft that as a docs PR against microsoft/playwright, or as an
  issue first to gauge their reaction before writing the PR?

✻ Cogitated for 46s

❯ Yes and what will you say first tell me then we will publish                  

● Here's the draft — I'll show it in full before anything gets published:

  ---
  Title: Page.content() omits shadow DOM, but locators pierce it — selectors
  that look unique silently aren't (revives #30816)

  Body:

  Problem

  Page.content() only serializes light DOM — shadow root contents are invisible
  to it, which is expected per the DOM serialization spec. But Playwright's own
  CSS-based locators pierce open shadow roots by default when matching at
  runtime.

  That mismatch is a real footgun for anything that builds selectors or
  automation from page.content()'s output (scrapers, selector generators, AI
  browser agents, test recorders): a CSS path that looks unique in the
  light-DOM-only HTML can still match a second element inside an open shadow
  root — silently, since matching succeeds and no error is raised. The result is
  a click (or any locator action) landing on the wrong element,
  indistinguishable from a correct one.

  Minimal repro

  html = """<html><body>
    <div id="host"><button onclick="mark('light')">Light</button></div>
    <script>
      window.hits = [];
      function mark(v) { window.hits.push(v) }
      host.attachShadow({mode: 'open'}).innerHTML =
        "<button onclick=\"window.hits.push('shadow')\">Shadow</button>";
    </script>
  </body></html>"""

  page.set_content(html)

  # Looks unique in page.content() — the shadow button is invisible to it.
  assert page.content().count("<button") == 1

  # But Playwright's own locator pierces the shadow root and finds BOTH:
  assert page.locator("#host > button").count() == 2  # not 1

  A tool that parses page.content(), sees one <button>, and hands back #host > 
  button as "the unique selector" has just produced one that silently matches
  two live elements.

  This was already proposed, and closed for inactivity — not rejected on merit

  #30816 asked for exactly this (page.content({ serializableShadowRoots: true })
  via Element.getHTML({ serializableShadowRoots: true })) and was closed after
  ~16 months with:

  ▎ "If you have additional information not present in this issue that you think
  ▎ will help prioritizing it, please open a new issue and reference this one. 
  ▎ More support or clarity on its necessity may prompt a review."

  This is that — a concrete failure mode instead of a general "let the crawler
  grab everything," plus proof it's buildable.

  Already built and validated, independently

  Hit this building zerodom (an MCP server generating verified-unique CSS
  selectors for AI agents), and implemented exactly what #30816 proposed —
  walking the DOM for open shadow roots, serializing them via Element.getHTML({ 
  serializableShadowRoots: true, shadowRoots }), and feeding the result back
  into selector generation so it can see (and correctly disambiguate against)
  shadow content it would otherwise be blind to.

  Validated across 111 real sites — 1,334 of 10,756 audited selectors had to be
  scoped against open shadow roots (121 of 129 on shoelace.style alone), every
  one resolving to exactly one live element once shadow content was actually
  visible.

  Ask

  Not asking for that exact implementation — just that Page.content() (or a
  documented alternative) makes shadow-root content visible, so tools built on
  it stop silently producing selectors that look correct and aren't. Happy to
  share the full implementation/tests, or help however's useful given how
  content() is actually implemented across Chromium/Firefox/WebKit.

  ---
  How's that read? Anything you want cut, softened, or reworded before it goes
  up?
✻ Brewed for 28s

❯ do we have to solve this also i mean we can                                   

● No — and I'd actively recommend not attempting the PR yet. Two reasons:

  1. The maintainer explicitly asked for "more support or clarity," not code.
  That's a lower bar to clear than a working cross-engine implementation, and
  it's the exact thing that revives a stalled issue.
  2. A real PR here is a bigger lift than it looks. Page.content() is
  implemented separately for Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit inside Playwright's
  own codebase, plus their Python/JS/Java/C# language bindings all need to
  expose the same option consistently. Zerodom's own implementation only proves
  the Chromium path works (via getHTML()) — Firefox/WebKit support for that API
  may lag or differ, and I haven't dug into Playwright's actual source to know
  where this needs to plug in. Submitting a big cross-engine PR cold, with zero
  prior maintainer signal on whether they even want it done that way, risks a
  lot of wasted work for nothing.

  So: file the issue, let it get a real response, and then decide whether to
  build the PR — with actual direction from someone who knows their codebase's
  internals, instead of guessing. We can absolutely do the implementation later;
  we just don't have to gamble it upfront.

● That screenshot exposes real, previously undocumented issues — not just the

  happening on those broken nodes:

● Something more significant than the known canvas gap: real DOM buttons visible
  in the screenshot (the Restaurants/Hotels/Transit pill row, the alert's
  Review button, zoom controls) aren't in the graph at all — that's not a
  documented limitation. Let me investigate why before drawing conclusions,
  using zerodom_eval_js to check the actual DOM state:

  Called zerodom 3 times (ctrl+o to expand)

● Strong hypothesis: this is a timing issue, not a traversal bug —
  zerodom_parse_url waits for domcontentloaded, but Maps is a heavy async SPA
  that likely finishes rendering its real UI after that event fires. Let's test
  by re-reading the same page without reloading, now that time has passed:

  Called zerodom (ctrl+o to expand)

● Found real things — one meaningful gap, two genuine bugs, and confirmation of
  an already-known limitation. Here's what testing Google Maps (never
  benchmarked before) turned up:

  1. A real gap vs. Playwright: timing. First parse (zerodom_parse_url, waits
  for domcontentloaded) found only 10 nodes. A re-read of the same, unreloaded
  page seconds later found 39 — the Restaurants/Hotels/Transit category pills,
  zoom controls, Layers, Menu/Saved/Recents, search suggestions, all invisible
  to the first parse. Root cause: domcontentloaded fires on initial HTML parse,
  but Maps is a heavy client-hydrated SPA that keeps mounting real UI well after
  that event. This is a structural consequence of zerodom's design —
  Playwright's own page.locator() auto-waits and re-queries at click time, so a
  raw Playwright script doesn't have this failure mode the same way an upfront
  full-page snapshot does.

  2. A real, reproducible bug: icon-font glyphs leaking into labels. Verified
  via eval_js that these aren't shadow-DOM, aren't hidden, aren't iframed —
  they're plain, real, visible buttons. But their labels come out as
  '\ue56cRestaurants', '\ue32cGet app', '\ue866Saved' — Google's icon-font
  ligature characters (Unicode Private Use Area, U+E000–U+F8FF) getting
  concatenated straight into the label text alongside the real word. Those
  codepoints are never meaningful text; they're a browser-rendering convention
  for icon fonts. Cheap, safe fix: strip the PUA range in label_linker.py's
  text-extraction path.

  3. A real bug: <body> misclassified as a clickable button. node_01 has type: 
  body, role: button, action: click, with its label literally being raw inline
  <script> source text ("tick('b0');if (window.devicePixelRatio > 1)...").
  <body> should essentially never be an actionable node — this is a parser
  fallback picking up a script tag's text content as if it were the body's own
  label.

  4. Minor: a redundant node. A wrapper <div> around the 7 category-pill buttons
  is also listed as its own clickable node with all 7 children's text mashed
  together — double-counted alongside the buttons it contains.

  Confirmed, not new: the entire map canvas — hundreds of clickable POI pins,
  pan/zoom-by-drag — is completely invisible to the graph, exactly matching
  ROADMAP.md's already-documented canvas/WebGL limitation.

  Want me to fix #2 and #3 now (both small, real, low-risk parser fixes with
  clear regression tests), or just log these as findings for later?

● All fixes verified live against the real, previously-untested Google Maps
  page:

  Summary of the hour's work — all 4 issues fixed, tested, and confirmed live:

  1. Hydration timing gap — zerodom_parse_url now does domcontentloaded + a
  bounded (2s), best-effort networkidle top-up (_goto() in mcp_server.py). Live
  result: first parse went from 10 nodes → 28 nodes in one call
  (Restaurants/Hotels/Transit pills, Set location, Menu/Saved/Recents/Get app,
  Google account links — all captured immediately, no second call needed). One
  run only reached 8, confirming this is honestly a best-effort improvement, not
  a hard guarantee — zerodom_read_page remains the documented fallback for very
  heavy SPAs.
  2. PUA icon-glyph pollution — label_linker.py's text_of() now strips
  Private-Use-Area codepoints (U+E000–F8FF). Live labels now read clean:
  'Restaurants', 'Get app', 'Saved' — no more '\ue56cRestaurants'-style garbage.
  3. <body> misclassified as clickable, with raw <script> text as its label —
  root-caused to tabindex="-1" being treated as an interactivity signal (it
  means "focusable via .focus() only," never a real control per the HTML spec).
  Fixed in parser.py's _is_interactive. Live result: the <body> node with
  "tick('b0');if (window.devicePixelRatio > 1)..." as its label is completely
  gone.
  4. Redundant wrapper node (the 7-pill container double-counted alongside its
  own children) — same root cause as #3, same fix. Live result:
  Restaurants/Hotels/Transit/etc. now appear as 7 clean, individual nodes with
  no duplicate wrapper.

  Also hardened text_of() against <script>/<style> text leaking into any label,
  not just body's — defense-in-depth beyond the one confirmed instance.

● Important correction to be transparent about: I checked lxml's built-in
  .text_content() directly, and it already excludes comment text by default
  ('Join', not '?lit$123$Join') — per the DOM textContent spec. So the
  comment-leak bug wasn't a pre-existing zerodom defect at all; it was a
  regression I introduced myself earlier this session, when I replaced the
  built-in .text_content() with my own hand-rolled _text_content_excluding()
  walker (to add script/style exclusion) — that walker naively recursed into
  Comment nodes' .text, where the original method correctly skipped them. Good
  that it's caught and fixed now, but it should be reported honestly as "found
  and fixed my own regression," not as an independent pre-existing bug.

  posts beneath it. Let's fix it:
